I will give an overview of this debate in the Commission. Riina Sikkut presented the purpose and content of the draft. He explained that the Socats have already made proposals to increase the unfairly low tax-free income, twice in total during the composition of the Riigikogu. This is considered to be crucial for managing low-income households. That is the position of the draft representative. He also said that the draft would raise the income limit, from which onwards tax-free income would start to decrease according to the calculation formula. While it is currently the case that tax-free income is starting to decrease from an annual income of EUR 14400, i.e. a monthly income of EUR 1200, the draft states that the tax-free part will decrease from a monthly income of EUR 2000 or EUR 24000 per year. It is also proposed that the rate of decline should be higher than in the past. If the current Act has a tax-free income of EUR 25200 per year 0, the draft provides that the tax-free income is 0 if there is a monthly income of EUR 3180, i.e. EUR 38160 per year. The aim of the tax reduction is also to stabilise the labour market. As a result of the high price increase, a sharp increase in the price of products and services is predicted. In fact, we already have it, we submitted this bill months ago. Any price increases will reduce consumption. For companies, the surge in input prices at the same time and the decreasing sales turnover mean pressure to find savings at the expense of the workforce. In other words, companies are under pressure to lay off their employees. Tax reductions therefore support businesses and help to maintain jobs and thus stabilise our labour market. The draft consists of three paragraphs; it is not a very complex draft. The first paragraph sets out changes to the rates of excise duties on fuels. The second paragraph sets out changes to VAT rates and extends the preferential rate of VAT to fuels. The third paragraph is the date of entry into force. Paragraph 1 (1) of the Bill reduces excise duties on petrol, diesel, gas and fuel oil. For all of these, the principle is very simple: the rates of excise duty will be reduced to the minimum rates set by the EU regulation. Paragraph 2 (1) and (2) of the draft provides for a new preferential rate of VAT of 5% instead of 9% and adds the fuels subject to the rate of excise duty to the list of goods subject to VAT. There's a very important couple of nuances here. First of all, we are fulfilling the promise that Ansip has not made so far. When VAT was temporarily increased to 9% in 2009, first by 5% and then by 18% to 20%, the promise was that if times were to improve, it would be brought down again. So far, times have not improved. But another very nice nuance is that the government has brought us the [proposal] to amend the VAT law, the VAT rate being reduced from 9% to 5% for the press. The press is also covered in our section, but it does not receive special treatment, but everyone else who is under this preferential rate will receive 5% instead of 9%. This means, among other things, that the Estonian state does not create a new tax corridor, where we have 20% VAT, 9% VAT and 5% VAT, but we have two tax corridors, one is 20% and the other is 5%. Just a little bit more of the effects. Reducing excise duty rates will have a positive impact on companies' activities, as it will be possible to reduce fuel prices at the expense of excise duties. The competitiveness of Estonian entrepreneurs compared to the main trading partners will also improve, the livelihood of Estonian people and the relative purchasing power will improve. The impact of these tax changes or reductions is undoubtedly extensive. In Estonia, the cost of motor fuel, as well as home-made fuel, will be more favourable and the amount of people's compulsory expenditure will be significantly reduced. It also significantly reduces costs for businesses. The impact on production companies is particularly significant. We ourselves calculate that if we lower the rates of VAT and excise duties, this could lead to a reduction in inflation of at least 2% to 3%. In the case of 19% where we are now, this may seem a little small. However, when we carried out an impact analysis of this draft, we did not yet have the current inflation statistics, so the actual impact is probably significantly higher. Its impact on consumer groups is also very extensive, in essence affecting all people, all consumer groups, as well as entrepreneurship and production. I would also like to point out that the impact is also regional. This has a positive regional impact as transport costs are higher in sparsely populated areas. A significant part of the food industry and food production are also located in sparsely populated rural areas. If we also want to reverse or suppress the pressure on food prices, we can also achieve this through this. Of course, critics point out the possibility that these tax reductions will not actually reach the final price. Here, however, I would like to point out that the exact same story was given to us when we reduced excise duty on alcohol and diesel excise. In both cases, the tax reductions reached a final price, both of which achieved exactly that objective: border trade disappeared, tax receipts actually increased, domestic production of these specific sectors recovered and tax receipts recovered. It is very important here, of course, to keep in mind that the state should urge all those affected by this tax reduction to introduce this tax reduction into prices. Now a little bit of the impact on the state budget. The reduction of excise duties on fuel will certainly lead to a reduction in excise revenue, which is clear. The reduction of the preferential rate of VAT and the extension of the preferential rate will also reduce the revenue from the state budget. However, we can see clearly that we have already seen this by statistics and by the receipts from the state budget in the first quarters of this year, that the overall increase in prices has resulted in a significant increase in VAT receipts. The revenue from the sale of CO2 quotas has also increased significantly in the country's income. The initiator of the draft legislation estimates that excise revenue will decrease this year if we implement... Well, we can no longer implement it from April, as our original plan was, but we will probably be able to implement it either 30 June or at least half a year, that is, 1 July. In this case, the impact on the state budget for this year is estimated to be EUR 80 million less on the excise series and EUR 130 million next year. The reduction of VAT and the extension of the preferential rate is estimated to reduce revenue by EUR 70 million in 2022 and by EUR 120 million in 2023. However, the revenue from other VAT already exceeds the estimated receipts from the state budget in the current year by more than EUR 200 million. This trend we can clearly see from the current receipts. True, if the economy collapses completely in the coming months, the amount may not be that high, but it is clear from current trends that VAT will certainly be several hundred million more than was predicted when the budget was adopted. However, it can be seen even more clearly that the revenue from the sale of CO2 to the state budget exceeds EUR 300 million. This overcharge is also not planned in the state budget. Thus, the estimated over-charges in relation to the revenue planned for the budget are more than twice as high as the under-loss resulting from our tax reduction, and the tax reductions implemented in this draft therefore do not lead to a budget deficit. As a result, there is no need to change the state budget. But, of course, if there is any will, we can also make some adjustments in the state budget we are dealing with. I must, of course, conclude by saying that the life of forecasters has not been easy in recent years: the corona and now there are different influences, the effects of war. But our forecasters, whether they've been at the central bank or the Ministry of Finance, are still very, very boldly misplaced by these numbers, with hundreds of millions of dollars, have missed. So I think that the numbers that I have just put out here are no less serious than the forecasts of some institutions, rather they are more serious. But I'm gonna finish my introductory speech and I'll be happy to answer the questions.

These common things, of course, include the creation of structures that guarantee free competition and adequate supply in the market. It is also part of helping those who are really in trouble, but it does not belong to being paid for, given up taxes, and then trying to create universal prosperity for everyone. The Commission certainly does not think that it is the party that can carry out impact assessments itself and say that we think we do. No, the party is not "we ourselves" in society, in a free society it is not the one who says that inflation is in our hands and a few or three percent and even more we are now reducing it. There are some further arguments that the majority of the Commission certainly disagree with. "This bill does not create a budget deficit. "Can't be created, the budget deficit is already heavy, the magnitude is 4% of GDP in the face of crises. However, we do have data that the annual deficit increase will have an impact of 100 million. This information is in the government document. If anyone wants, I can specify this table, it's not just going to be read out here. We don't think we're making predictions, and then we'll say everything's been missed if it doesn't happen. We certainly do not agree with the Commission that if the right party is in power, prices will fall, and if there is a wrong party, prices will rise. It still depends on political choices and external circumstances. The fall in prices during the pandemic was certainly not a service of the Minister of Finance; his actions were still aimed at increasing prices, but the pandemic was a dominant factor. The supply difficulties that arose at the time and the fall in demand were the ones that undercut prices. "Early Difficulty" may be an inaccurate word, but basically the demand went down because there were restrictions, couldn't demand, couldn't buy, couldn't consume. After the rise in prices, the price increase still had the assumption that these restrictions had withdrawn, inflation started to accelerate and, of course, it got in when Putin started shutting down the gas screens with the deployment of troops to the Ukrainian border, with the deployment of new troops. So I don't think as capable as a man behind my back that he could have done a lot of damage. Of his policy options, on which he said they would affect inflation, it really occurred to me that, on this point, I think my colleague has already asked that this early payout certainly increases inflation is one of those internal factors. The budget deficit created at a time of good economic growth, which he himself has welcomed in every way, has also been able to create it, certainly increases inflation, i.e. inflation. These are the policy options. But I don't think he could have been the reason to make a trade deficit. The Commission in general thinks that this is too cheap a goal set, when I try here to generalise our various debates, that things must be cheap. No, that is not an end in itself. This is why excise duties are used, for example, to increase the price of goods with harmful side effects. In turn, money collected from it is essential to alleviate pain for the weaker and investment in social terms. But the claim that we are ensuring prosperity by abolishing consumption taxes is a felt, and we are also ensuring a fall in prices. There is a direct link that if we reduce consumption taxes, we will stimulate demand. In this sense, it is not a linear effect towards a fall in prices. Statistical comparisons show that those who have reduced consumption taxes will not achieve any lasting fall in prices, but will be a temporary decrease in prices, which will then be smoothed out, and the trend will recover. The people of the Committee on Monetary Affairs have seen much of this. As far as energy is concerned, what is said about it is that, in the case of energy prices, the price increase is one clear reason that supply is too low for various reasons. The longer view is that investments were not made in Europe at the right time, but the second [cause] is still that we are gradually abandoning Russian energy carriers. This deficit-induced inflation cannot in any way be controlled by the fact that we are stimulating demand. No, we still have to increase the offer if the price increase is due to it. Eesti Pank has tried to explain this to us. It is a pity that the rapporteur is not in contact with the finances, does not know the reasons, and makes up his own impact analyses and forecasts and makes very loose assessments here. So, yes, the state needs taxes to help the weaker. But if he starts losing taxes, it's not enough that he's left less money for investments, for investment grants, he's also going to support those who can consume more. It is the devil in reducing consumption taxes that this victory is distributed to those who already have and who do not really need aid. This is the general rule and has been the case so far with policy analyses. But, of course, here we heard all sorts of fairy tales that didn't go through in the Committee on Monetary Affairs. Our task is to provide greater security to the people of Estonia during this anxious period than ever before. In the autumn, we saw the threat posed by the Belarus hybrid attack. We showed by our real actions that the surveillance of the Estonian border begins at the Lithuanian-Belarus border, where we sent an ESTPOL-5 police unit to help prevent mass illegal immigration on the Lithuanian-Belarus border. We offered important support to our neighbours, including Latvia and Poland. I am sure that Estonia will never be alone in a similar situation. On the 104th anniversary of Estonia's independence Putin Russia's full-scale military invasion of an independent Ukrainian state began. Although the war is not far from us, Estonia is safe. Our public order and values are protected. A strong and functioning network of allies will help protect us. On the other hand, in a difficult security situation, we must assess the state's readiness with a completely new perspective and be prepared for all kinds of scenarios. Good listeners! The security of the Estonian state is based on a strong border, which is better guarded today than ever before. Our goal is a well-protected external border and our goal is to build our border infrastructure fully by 2025. This is a signicant and important step in the whole of Europe's security perspective, as the Schengen area as a whole is even safer. Estonia is an example and a worthy partner for many countries here. Thanks to the decisions to allocate additional funds, we have been able to raise the ambition of building the border. For example, we can build a fence on the land border where we had abandoned it earlier because of the limited budget, and also build patrols and surveillance positions on the Narva River. We have also made efforts to bring the deadline for border construction up to and including one and a half years closer, to 2025 instead of the end of 2026. The migratory pressure that has been artificially created on Lithuania, Latvia and Poland has shown us that, alongside border construction, there is a need to amend the state border law so that the police can also do their job in situations where foreigners are used as a weapon in a hybrid attack. Work on this is ongoing and the corresponding draft is currently under the procedure of the Riigikogu. Looking back into the past for a second, we are not going to get over or around the COVID-19 crisis. This has also had a significant impact on the area of internal security. The society is divided by conspiracy, false news, disinformation spread quickly on the web. This also increased the workload of law enforcement authorities, especially web-constabilities. This year we have also experienced the spread of false information and propaganda because of the war launched by Putin in Ukraine. We must therefore be prepared for all kinds of, and different, crises. According to last year's survey, 77% of municipalities have achieved advanced or exemplary levels of crisis preparedness. According to the emergency preparedness survey of residents, people's readiness in parts of Estonia has improved over a few years, although it is lower in Ida-Virumaa, for example. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Here we need a joint contribution from all of us to reach all the corners of Estonia and all the people of Estonia. Making preparations for crises is, of course, better at a peaceful time, but decisions with greater impact are often made when crises are tangible, whether it is driven by a massive blackout, a blizzard, a pandemic, mass immigration or, in this case, the war in Ukraine. Thus, the 'Estonia 2035' action plan must continue to be a strong direction to strengthen crisis preparedness, and so must be supranational. Within a little more than a year, fundamental decisions have been made in a broad national defence perspective in the area of the Ministry of the Interior's administration of nearly 140 million euros and some funds have already been devoted to activities. This clearly shows that protection and security of our population have a very important role to play in national defence. Funding must form a coherent whole in the future. With the additional money, we can now resolve a number of previously pending concerns and take several good steps forward to strengthen the country's preparedness. The area of government of the Ministry of the Interior focuses, for example, on increasing the protection of the population so that Estonian people can cope better in crisis situations. The preparation of shelters that have received much attention, risk awareness sessions, as well as outreach, risk communication and, of course, exercises will contribute to this. We plan to develop both collapse-savings, chemical rescues and demining capabilities. The Rescue Board will continue to provide guidance and training for local authorities in improving crisis preparedness. The plan is to further develop a pool of volunteers with a crisis role. We will also continue with the cooperation agreement concluded by the Minister of the Interior and the Minister of Defence in 2021 in order to increase cooperation between the fields of internal defence and military defence and in domestic crisis management. It will, inter alia, help to clarify how the Defence Forces and the Defence League can be involved in crisis resolution and to clarify the role of crisis volunteers. The Ministry of the Interior, in cooperation with the authorities in the area of government, has launched a number of prevention and outreach activities to improve preparedness for crises, such as the "Varu before the Maru" campaign. We are also preparing for the introduction of a location-based notification system for mobile phones already at the end of this year. This will help citizens and institutions to report the risks in a timely manner. For public authorities, clarification of the allocation of roles was probably the most important in the area of crisis regulation. The preparations for civil crises and national defence were united last year into one whole, coordinated by the Government of the Republic and the State Chancellery. This will allow the country as a whole to be prepared for different simultaneous crises, in which each institution solves its own responsibilities, even in a crisis situation. Whether it is a crisis or not, every person is helped to make Estonia safer. A difficult time has shown that every person needs to think about what are the factors that can have a significant impact on his/her life, his/her family, as well as on the daily lives of the community, such as the sighting of a needy person, increasing the security of his/her home, the provision of possible crises and, of course, the prevention of accidents with responsible behaviour. Useful tips for dealing with crisis situations can also be found in the Women's Home Protection app and on the Rescue Board's website "Be Ready!'. Thus, prevention is very important in the "Estonia 2035" view; it is one of the best protections against potential threats. A safe environment is created by proactive behaviour. The state must play a leading role here, but every person must contribute to a secure environment. I will bring out some prevention activities that the Ministry of the Interior and its administration have consistently carried out. We have various programs, outreach activities, as well as home counselling. To give a separate example of the prevention of close relationship violence. Last year, we tried the method of pro-active conversation directed at those who were violent. Its aim is to prevent the recurrence of violence. This is worth noting, in particular, because it provides for the first time an opportunity to assist all sides of violence. Often, the origins of the problems that cause accidents are the same. After all, social and health problems are interrelated, and prevention must therefore be carried out across sectors. To this end, we agreed, for example, with the Minister for Justice, the Minister for Education and Science, the Minister for Culture and the Minister for Social Protection on the principles of cross-sectoral prevention. Another concrete example of the systematicisation of cooperation in prevention is the approval of "Estonian drug policy 2030" by the Government of the Republic's Committee on Drugs Prevention. It is, of course, more important than documents to take concrete steps. Local authorities and local networking also play an important role in prevention and security. Security networking has been being carried out for years. It now needs to move forward meaningfully, and for that we are drawing up the concept of the councils for the security of local governments. This year we plan various activities to support local governments. However, how can local authorities contribute to the role of improving and preventing security? The amendments to the Traffic Act, which entered into force this March, are a good example. This allows municipalities, in cooperation with the Police and Border Guard Board and the Transport Department, to install speed cameras on the local road, i.e. to be involved in the enforcement of traffic. Half of the amount of traffic camera fines will be received by the municipality that installed the camera in the future and the resulting revenue will be used by the municipalities in particular to solve problems affecting traffic safety. This has certainly been the right course. As we speak here today about the future of internal security, it is also important to raise the question of whether, in addition to the police, the circle of security providers could be wider. Catching a dangerous criminal is, of course, the work of the police, and so it will remain. On the other hand, the police have tasks that might be performed, for example, by the law enforcement authorities of the local government, if the local government has the willingness and will to do so, not that it is a duty. If there is readiness, then there will certainly be a question of cost recovery, and here too, the state has a role to play. Here, for example, the Netherlands: local law enforcement officers are taken there as officials with limited police rights and their tasks include, for example, dealing with minor offences. As I pointed out above, in recent years we have had to resolve various crises where the police have been at the forefront. Both the municipal law enforcement units and the security company have always been very supportive of the police during this period and have done very well with their tasks. The fact is that the number of police officers is now the smallest ever and the security crisis, I dare say, the greatest ever. It's a sore spot that definitely needs to be solved. Thus, expanding the range of security service providers would bring more people into the field, help to share the burden and create a power arm and would help to ensure the well-being and safety of the population. You've probably all heard the saying that people are our greatest asset. This is indeed the case in the area of internal security and internal security. Society highly appreciates their work, and this is demonstrated by people's trust in police, rescue and alarm personnel. Namely, it is still the three highest-trusted institutions in Estonia. However, this alone is far from sufficient to keep employees. We are constantly trying to improve the working environment as well as development and career opportunities, but we cannot escape the issue of pay. The problem is with minimum wages for police, rescue and rescue officers. Last year it was possible to raise the minimum wage by 11.5% for police officers, 11.4% for rescue workers and 6.4% for rescue agents, thanks to the additional money received from the state budget and the internal security authorities. But that is far from enough. Yes, in order to keep wages competitive, internal security and internal security agencies have and will continue to restructure their activities wherever possible. On the other hand, internal restructuring is no longer enough. Let me give you an example here. If, for example, the necessary wage level should be ensured from the so-called own resources of the institutions, we would be forced to lay off half of the staff of the Police and Border Guard Board and the Rescue Board by 2025. This is not conceivable, because such concessions in terms of speed of assistance, saving lives and property cannot be allowed in any way. The number of internal security staff has also decreased by 18% since 2008 and a large retiring wave is ahead for both police and rescue officers. Thus, the major challenge for the coming years will be to provide decent remuneration in the field of internal security and to ensure that new staff are trained to the extent necessary. More public budget resources will be needed in these areas in the future. I stress that securing and creating internal security is not just a cost. Last year we measured the contribution of the Rescue Board and the Police and Border Guard Board to society. According to this, the Rescue Board will generate revenue for society of nearly EUR 1 billion per year and the Police and Border Guard Board of EUR 867 million per year. Thus, these figures, the contribution of internal security institutions to society exceeds several times the money allocated to them in the state budget. I would point out that these are not fines. Alongside the reacting institutions, our more than 4,000 volunteers are playing a major role in internal security, and our excellent people, who have the indispensable power. Volunteers respond to accidents, provide a backside for rescue work, ensure traffic safety, contribute to traffic surveillance activities and preventative work. In 2021, volunteers were added to the ranks of rescuers and marine rescuers. Volunteers are also helping to answer 1247 calls on the national hotline. Although the number of relief officers decreased by 4%, the number of hours worked by relief officers increased thanks to their diverse involvement. In addition to increasing the number of volunteers in internal security, we will continue to work towards more diverse volunteering and participation opportunities for existing volunteers. We must be grateful because the more people with different backgrounds and experiences act as volunteers, the better solutions will be found, the more successful we will be in preventing accidents in the field of internal security in the future and in providing rapid assistance. In order to ensure the highest possible level of internal security, we have considered it important to modernise the working conditions of the creators and keepers of internal security. Over the last year, our employees have moved to several new or renovated buildings. Some good examples: the Pärnu joint building, the Sillamäe joint building, the Valga police station, the joint buildings of the Defence Police Department, Kohtla-Järve and Kiviõli and the new-looking rescue commando buildings are being completed for Türi and Ruhnu island. There are many other buildings in the design and order: for Kilingi-Nõmme, Viljandi, Jüri, Haapsalu, Paide and Jõgeva commandos. An important step in this direction is a significant improvement in the follow-up of internal security staff and in the opportunities for further training. We have decided that we will expand the training opportunities in the Väike-Maarja exercise square of the Estonian Academy of Interior Protection. These are all important investments so that we can provide even more quality assistance in the future. A few words also include changes to legislation that will help to create internal security more efficiently and smartly and to keep it today and tomorrow. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! With your help, we have adopted a law that allows biometric personal data to be occupied and preserved. This will enable us, for example, to fight crime more effectively, to ensure security in society and to protect personal data with modern, high-level tools. By law of foreigners, we pay serious attention to preventing and preventing the misuse of migration, both in labour and learning mobility, on the other hand, we encourage investment in Estonia. In addition, in this Chamber, the draft harmonisation of special services that you have just approved, concerning the various social guarantees and service regulations for internal security workers and volunteers. The development of a new Preparedness Act is underway, which will make the roles in preparing for civil crises and national defence even clearer. Internal security authorities are the first line of defence and therefore a clear legal framework and broad-based cooperation in both preparation and resolution are essential. However, the work field is wide. To improve the system of issuing identity documents, we work in the direction of finding a service provider who, in cooperation with the Police and Border Guard Board, would issue identity documents both in Estonia and Finland. We also work with alternative solutions to install digital kiosks outside the service halls, which would help bring the services closer to the people of Estonia, so that they do not always have to go to the police service hall. Then, for example, the PBGB should not necessarily go to the service room to take a document photo or to give fingerprints or follow a document. Finally, I will also briefly open up issues in the area of civil society. One of the important challenges facing us in the 'Estonia 2035' view is that local authorities cooperate systematically with local residents and communities on a network basis. A large part of the citizens' initiatives take place at local level, where people often gain their first experience in participating in civil society. At the same time, there is a lack of systematic cooperation at local level with local residents and communities. It is important to create an interest and willingness in communities across Estonia to participate in local life issues and to create the necessary forms of cooperation for this purpose. We are planning, for example, this year to develop a model for a community-centred approach. It would also certainly be better to improve the legal framework, for example, to create opportunities for donors to financially support not only registered NGOs but also so-called informal citizens' initiatives or to define more clearly local authorities' tasks as networking communities in the region. This will all contribute to the development of communities. The stronger our communities are, the stronger our country will be. The Ministry of the Interior is also a party to the Social Business and Innovation Cooperation Agreement, which was signed yesterday. Its aim is to confirm the interest of all parties to engage in the holistic development of sectoral organisations and social innovation. It will also be one of the priorities of the coming years to set up a fund to support innovative and innovative civil society initiatives. Our task is to create a favourable environment and fertile soil for civil society, so that all of this can actually be done. Let's finish with a positive note. Significant progress was made in participating in the charity of Estonian residents. Between 2015 and 2020 donations to charity increased by 58%, with the share of permanent donors in the population being 19% last year. In conclusion, we have been living together in crises for several years, but thanks to the good people of Estonia, we have successfully managed with them and by keeping them together. Why do I say "together"? Because in crises, the contribution of every person, company, organization, local government and many others counts. The area of government of the Ministry of Internal Affairs is working every day to ensure that the sense of security in Estonia is preserved and that people's awareness of their crisis role is raised. We achieve a safe living environment as a result of the joint work, care and responsibility of everyone. Thank you for listening. I'm ready to answer questions.

Indeed, in this sense, when we look at the Ukrainians who are fleeing from the war, they are, of course, more crossing the border, namely, across the external borders in the same way.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-05-11_U353	Thank you. I take the opportunity of my second question and will continue on this subject: the Ukrainian refugees coming across the Russian border. ParlaMint-EE_2022-05-11_U354	Thank you. Indeed, when we talk about external border surveillance, I say that external border control is very strong, very effective. Very many people who want to enter Estonia from the Russian Federation will be denied entry and will be rerouted. There are more than five, more than ten people each day who cannot cross the border because they cannot explain the purpose of the journey and their entry into the country is prohibited. This amount is quite large a day, not only on the land border, but also on the air border at Tallinn airport, for example. Basic control is not so-called this transit control, because indeed, if a person has already entered the European Union, then progress is free from here, we do not have border controls, no one controls a person when we leave Estonia, we are checking the way in at the southern border today. But once again: at the external border, this control is very active, interviews are being conducted, and a very large number of people are being directed to the second stage, where further checks are being carried out on these people. Indeed, there have also been cases when a person has two passports with him, for example, and then there is a different story, and a person cannot get here. So I can assure you that controls at the external borders are very strong.

A draft law amending the Government of the Republic Act and other laws, presented before you by the Government of the Republic, aims to enhance the acquisition of material resources in support of the state's military defence, to accelerate the mobilisation of state assets by the emergency management authority during special arrangements and to clarify the area of responsibility of the Ministry of Defence. In order to enhance the acquisition of material resources, the draft provides for the pooling of competence in the acquisition of the necessary resources in the National Defence Investment Centre. Insofar as expropriation and/or expropriation are permitted in addition to the normal acquisition of funds, it is necessary to empower the ICC to exercise public authority. To this end, the RIK will cease its activities as a state agency managed by the Ministry of Defence and the RIK will be transformed into a government agency authorised to exercise public authority, including the obligation to exercise public supervision. In addition to bringing together the competence to acquire assets in the IKK, the IKK is transferred to the planning coordination responsibilities, which are also linked to ensuring the operational capacity of the facilities managed by the IKKK. By concentrating tasks on the IKIC, a coherent overview of the material resources available in the country will be created, allowing better planning of their deployment, and providing an overview of the resources available. On the other hand, companies with military protection facilities or persons affected by defence objects will in the future be in contact with a single institution. This will speed up the search for the best solution for national security. The preparation and implementation of rapid and compact solutions is particularly important in today's security situation, where the threat of armed conflict is the greatest of the times of re-independence. As a result of the transfer of tasks related to the assignment of burdens, the Defence Resources Board, in short, will retain the KRA to organise the records and recruitment of human resources related to military defence. The concentration of similar activities in a single competence centre also reduces the number of overlapping activities. For example, when coordinating planning, the Ministry of Defence no longer has to ask the opinion of the RKI whether the planned object may affect the performance of the Defence Forces object and so on. In the Ministry of Defence's view, the policy-making process will improve as some of the operational activities will be diverted from the Ministry. As a result of the pooling of topics, the authorities can channel human resources to the core tasks and the result of this synergy is to increase preparedness for military defence. Seven different laws are amended as regards changes in the functions of the agencies. As a second amendment, I would point out the relaxation of the requirements for the redistribution of state assets. Reallocation of state assets is one of the tools to ensure that institutions involved in the settlement of special schemes have the necessary resources. Rapid use of resources will allow a rapid response and reduce the possibility of an event expanding. It should be noted here that the funds are not only movable property but also state-owned land. Under the current rules, the mobilisation of state assets is permitted only with the agreement of the asset manager. Such a requirement is not applicable in situations where the introduction of an asset is urgent. At this time, contact with the asset manager may not be possible. For example, the use of land to maintain the resources needed to deal with the flood and to manage the evacuation. In such an urgent and unavoidable event, the property may be used in the future before an agreement has been reached with the manager of the property. In addition to speeding up the take-up of state assets, the government will be given the opportunity to specify the arrangements for compensation for losses related to the use of state assets between the owners of state assets. The third amendment to the draft concerns the area of responsibility of the Ministry of Defence, as laid down in the Government of the Republic Act. The Riigikogu is dealing with a bill that adds to the tasks of the Defence Forces the surveillance of the maritime border and the detection and eradication of marine pollution, which was the task of the Police and Border Guard Board. Consequently, the draft also provides for the relevant area of responsibility to be brought to the administration of the Ministry of Defence. The RIK is also added to the list of institutions in the area of government of the Ministry of Defence. On behalf of the Government of the Republic, I propose to support the draft as it relates to developments in military defence and takes account of the established security situation today. If the honourable and honorable Riigikogu adopts this law, the date of entry into force would be 1 September 2022. Thank you for your attention.

The explanatory memorandum states that due to the changes, the sectoral situation in Estonia will increase and the ability to react quickly to the security situation in Europe and the world. Some words about procedural decisions. I was appointed rapporteur for the draft committee. ParlaMint-EE_2022-05-11_U466	Let's go to item number nine. This is the first reading of the draft law amending the Public Procurement Act and the Public Procurement Act and related amendments to other laws, initiated by the Government of the Republic. The rapporteur is Jaak Aab, Minister of State Administration. Please! How many changes did you have?
ParlaMint-EE_2022-05-11_U467	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! I would like to introduce you to this long name initiated by the Government of the Republic, which is due to the standard technique of the draft law, the content of which is amendments to the Public Procurement Act. Yes, now the question is why we come back with this proposal, only one package of changes went from here, but there are reasons for it. On 8 April, the Council of Europe adopted the fifth package of sanctions in connection with Russia's aggression in Ukraine. This package also included sanctions for public contracts. More specifically, a ban was introduced on the award of contracts to citizens, residents and businesses of the Russian Federation and to persons associated with them, falling within the scope of the directives on public procurement and prohibiting the continuation of contracts already concluded with those persons. The fifth package of sanctions entered into force on 9 April, which means that sanctions must already be applied in new public procurement as well as in those that started before that date. In addition, contracts awarded to persons subject to sanctions must be terminated. Although the Council of Europe Regulation imposing sanctions is directly applicable in all Member States of the European Union, it does not contain procedural rules on how to implement the regulation, which is why these procedural rules must be incorporated into national law. The clarification is that these standards are already in force because they are directly applicable. However, the Public Procurement Act, as well as the Law of Obligations Act, which deals with the termination of contracts, are too narrow for the correct implementation of the sanctions on public procurement in the fifth package. As European Union law prevails, internal law needs to be amended as a matter of urgency so that sanctions can be correctly and lawfully applied without major problems. Now, in principle or in essence, there are three problems that we are trying to solve with this draft by amending the Public Procurement Act. The first problem, the basis for elimination contained in the Public Procurement Act, according to which an economic operator who is himself or whose representative is subject to an international sanction is too narrow. This exclusion basis is covered by the provision of the Regulation under which no contract is awarded if the tenderer is himself a citizen of the Russian Federation, a resident or an undertaking established there. Two other points are not covered, i.e. where the subject of the sanction is the owner of the tenderer or candidate or the actual beneficiary, or represents or receives instructions from the subject of the sanction. An amendment to the Public Procurement Act, which enters into force on 1 June 2022, states that the contracting authority shall reject the tender if the award of the contract on the basis of that tender would infringe an international sanction within the meaning of the corresponding International Sanctions Act. While this will save the contracting authority from imposing such a condition in future public procurement, first, it will not solve the problem of ongoing public procurement and, second, it is not entirely correct since the rejection of the tender must be linked to the content of the tender rather than to the identity of the tenderer, as required by the Regulation. The sanctions provided for in the Regulation all relate to the identity of the tenderer. They should therefore be included in the grounds for disposal. As a solution to this problem, the draft amends the basis for removing sanctions and extends the application of both this amendment and the amendment which will enter into force on 1 June to public procurement already under way. This means that these provisions will be enforced retroactively. Problem number two. According to the Regulation, contracts awarded before 9 April, where a party is a party to a sanction, must be terminated by 10 October 2022. Contract termination takes place on the basis of the Law of Obligations Act. According to the Law of Obligations Act, it is possible to cancel durable contracts for an exceptional reason. Other contracts that are not long-term contracts can only be withdrawn in specific cases. However, these grounds for withdrawal cannot be applied to the current situation. It may therefore be that the Sanctions Regulation requires the termination of the agreement, but there is no legal basis for it at national level. This may in theory lead to a claim for damages for the contracting authority. On 1 June 2022, an amendment to the Public Procurement Act, which allows for the cancellation or withdrawal of contracts, shall enter into force if, during the performance of the contract, the economic operator has a basis for withdrawal. The problem, however, is that not all sanctions in the Regulation are covered by the grounds for elimination and the entry into force of the Public Procurement Act applies only to contracts awarded as a result of public procurement initiated after its entry into force. As a solution, we propose an amendment that amends the basis for lifting sanctions and extends the right to early termination of the contract to those contracts that have been awarded earlier, that is to say, the provisions will be enforced retroactively. Problem three. According to the International Sanctions Act, the Government of the Republic may also impose a sanction on the Government of the Republic to protect the security or interests of Estonia. If such a sanction is imposed in Estonia, this exclusion should also be included in the Public Procurement Act. The current Public Procurement Act does not deal specifically with the sanction of the Government of the Republic. While it is possible to conclude, in the combined interpretation of the relevant provisions of the Public Procurement Act and the International Sanctions Act, that the basis for the removal extends to the sanction of the Government of the Republic, a separate designation of the Government of the Republic sanction is necessary in order to prevent such complex and unnecessary interpretation processes. As a solution, the basis for lifting the sanction and the basis for rejecting the tenders will be amended and added that they will also apply to sanctions imposed by the Government of the Republic. In conclusion, the aim of the draft is to make the application of the Sanctions Regulation as free as possible for contracting authorities and to avoid a situation where the procurement procedure has to be annulled or the termination of the contract is followed by disputes with uncertain end in court. In addition, we have tried to formulate the amendments as widely as possible to cover the sanctions that will be imposed in the future. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-05-11_U487	The tenth item is the draft law amending the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. The rapporteur is Minister for Social Protection Signe Riisalo.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-05-11_U488	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! I am before you as a substitute for the Minister of Education and Science with amendments to the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act. There are two changes. The first amendment focuses on the activities of the Non-School Advisory Committee, which is regulated in today's Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act in such a way that all children with special educational needs are subject to compulsory and unavoidable evaluation and deviation from it by an educational specialist, social and health specialist. The fact is that children with special educational needs overlap with disabled children in one third of Estonia. Those children who have needed social evaluation, help and support, as well as health, are talking about psychiatric evaluation, have actually received this help and support in another system. This concern, which we have mostly, is the increase in the administrative burden on the public service provider, the various service providers, the Social Insurance Board, both the external evaluation committee and the local government, all of which have a role to play in the provision of services, to assess the child's need for assistance and to take an administrative decision based on it. However, excessive implementation of administrative procedures is the least of the problems. The bigger problem is the burden on parents and children, the winking between different authorities and assessors. The most infuriating thing is that often different assessment processes have been carried out in practical life and on the ground by the same specialists who are contract agents in different fields. Another concern is the overburdening of psychiatrists. We have very few psychiatrists, especially children's psychiatrists, and their implementation should be reasonable and necessary when a child has a serious need for help, so that this psychiatric resource, which we lack throughout Estonia, is not used where it is not really needed. And so the amendment is first of all true for children studying in Estonia, including children coming from Ukraine, to ensure adequate education and this on the recommendation of the non-school counselling team. The recommendation will involve social and health professionals, where appropriate, but should involve educational professionals. This is the first change. Now, the second amendment concerns only children from Ukraine. I am about to say that 34% of these 37039 refugees who have come to Estonia and remain here, as we know, are children. Those are the numbers this morning. 4448 children have started to study in Estonian educational institutions, including early childhood education institutions, and these children have the desire to provide additional post-secondary education, which is not part of the graduate education and is not yet part of upper secondary school studies, but provides the opportunity to study the language during this inter-annual period so that they can go on to regular education. These are the two changes. The second amendment is temporary; it is valid until the end of this year. The first change, which also concerns our own children, is intended to apply on a permanent basis. The entry into force of this draft would be in normal order, i.e. ten days after the publication in the State's Official Journal. This draft does not have specific costs for the admission of children to additional education. On the first point, it is understandable that these costs are already planned, and we are mitigating the situation. On the proposal of the Ministry of Education and Research, the Government of the Republic has already decided to grant additional support in the Ukrainian crisis according to the entries of the Estonian Education Information System, i.e. when we have the children of new immigrants on the school list at the beginning of the next academic year, the student's bounty will be awarded to the school. There is no additional need for separate resources in this draft. I'm ready to answer your questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-05-11_U526	Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Good minister! Good President! Indeed, on Tuesday, 3 May, the Committee on Culture discussed this bill. We discussed this as a fourth item on the agenda and the members of the Commission took part in the agenda [within] Helle-Moonika Helme, Jaak Juske, Jaanus Kariliid, who was Aadu Black's alternate, Signe Kivi, Anneli Ott, who was Marko Šorin's alternate, Heidy Purga, Üllar Saaremäe, Margit Sutrop and Aivar Viidik, who was Kristina Šmigun-Vähi's alternate. On this item, we had Liina Kersna, Minister of Education and Science in the Commission, and Indrek Kilk, Legal Adviser to the [Ministry of Justice] Legal Policy Department. I'm gonna give you a little bit of an overview of how we had this conversation. First of all, Liina Kersna introduced the draft, on which she said that both of its points were due to the need to support the children of the Ukrainian refugee war. The education system has reached over 4,000 Ukrainian children, it was on 3 May. Today we heard more specific numbers from Signe Riisalo. Indeed, there are over 4,000 children and most of them have entered general education schools. Children have come to Estonia who have graduated from the ninth grade on the basis of the national curriculum of Ukraine and have received a corresponding certificate from there. However, this certificate does not give them the opportunity to go to Estonian upper secondary schools and vocational schools, as our national curricula are different. It is therefore necessary to provide students with a flexible additional year of study during which language learning and fine-tuning training can be provided in subjects that have been identified as lagging behind. School leaders have agreed to recognise curricula in different countries. According to the lawyers of the Ministry of Education and Research, however, this is a law currently in force in Estonia, which deals only with the Estonian national curriculum, and therefore, under this current law, Ukrainian children cannot be offered a flexible additional year of study so that they can enter a secondary school or vocational school. At the moment, only students who have graduated from comprehensive school from Ukraine are affected by the amendment. According to the Minister, there may be a need to return to the subject of the additional academic year at a later stage, as in fact the problem is wider and concerns students from other countries as well. The second amendment to this draft is that I am talking about everything that the Minister of Education in the Commission has told us about non-school advisory committees. According to the current law, the external advisory team must, in any case, involve experts in the fields of education, health and social affairs. However, on the basis of the amendment to the draft, this external advisory team will only involve experts if necessary. The Minister explained that children's psychiatrists, for example, are overloaded and would rather seek advice from one child than from participating in the counselling team. But since the law now states that a health expert must in any case be involved, life has shown that these experts are formally involved. Many children with special educational needs have already been assessed, and this is a formal double. In addition, the child may not need to have a psychiatrist's assessment at all. The Minister stressed the importance of taking into account the needs of a specific child. He described here the whole process, a little more broadly, how this involvement of health and social experts is taking place. He admitted that the burden on the committees was high and that this burden would certainly increase in relation to the needs of the children of war that came from Ukraine. But he said that the corresponding amendment to the draft, which concerns these advisory committees, could remain in force on a permanent basis. The Minister also added that four Ukrainian psychologists will be recruited into the Pathfinder system, who will be helping out-of-school counselling teams to evaluate the children of Ukrainian refugees. Then the discussion started. Jaak Valge, who led the Commission's [session], started and said that he certainly does not question the need for further training, but whether a study has already been carried out or whether there is an intention to do so in order to find out what Ukrainian refugees expect of the Estonian state in more detail. Liina Kersna's reply made it clear that no studies had been carried out or were planned. He simply said that the additional year of study is intended to enable the child of Ukraine to enter the next level of education in Estonia if he or she so wishes. The focus of further education is on language learning, but other subjects will be combined if necessary. Jaak Valge asked whether the next year of additional education should be in Estonia. Liina Kersna replied that no one can be obliged to do this, but additional education creates a prerequisite for further study in Estonia and a secondary school can also be conducted. Jaak Valge advised the Ministry to conduct the study. And if, for example, the children of Ukraine are to pursue their education in the Ukrainian education system, there is no doubt that the need for further education should also be directed in a different way. Liina Kersna said that the additional year of study is entirely voluntary, but however, basic education is compulsory education in Estonia and all the children of Ukraine who have come to us should enter primary education. However, as far as the next level of education is concerned, each young person's own decision and the opportunity to continue studying in the Ukrainian system is also a matter of distance learning. I myself asked Liina Kersna that, as we know, the additional year of study in Ukraine will in any case mean the loss of one year to the child of war. From the perspective of upper secondary school [is] that Estonian children prepare for our upper secondary school for years, but they may not be able to get there. After all, this upper secondary school education is not compulsory and, therefore, receiving upper secondary school is not exactly the kind of thing that Ukraine should somehow allow for war refugees. I asked whether the Ukrainians would be granted separate quotas for the next level of education so that they would not lose this additional year of study. Liina Kersna replied that this was not a lost year, but that this year's training would take place. Kersna agreed that upper secondary school is not a place for everyone to enter. However, he said that the head of school has the right to impose special conditions for students from abroad, and this is a right granted to the head of school by law. In the case of new immigrants, this has also been assessed. If the selection panel assesses that the student's ability to study in upper secondary school is sufficient, but the Estonian language is poor, it will still be admitted to the upper secondary school and will help linguistically either with this additional year of study or on an ongoing basis. He also said that there was an agreement with the mayor of Tallinn that the state would take the seventh to ninth grade of both the progymnasium and the upper secondary school in Estonia's largest municipality and make the children of Ukraine a separate language immersion school from the autumn of this year. Jaak Valge asked if there was any information on the number of Ukrainian children in progymnasium and upper secondary school and whether there was a forecast of how many of them needed an additional year of study. Liina Kersna stated that, indeed, only half of the children of Ukraine in Estonia have reached the Estonian education system by now. By autumn, all these children must be found and brought into our education system. There are currently not many young people in upper secondary school who have reached Estonia. In particular, children from the first to sixth grade have arrived in Estonia. The Minister added that, to a large extent, data exists, and here he said the number that I was asking Minister Riisalo now, well, I suppose it was perhaps Liina Kersna's linguistic or some kind of glitch when she said that the need had been calculated up to 50,000 children. It's written in the record, that's how I read it. Margit Sutrop asked if it was possible to treat students from different vintages equally and whether an additional year could be adjusted for a student who had already started upper secondary school. Liina Kersna then said that the additional year of study can also be applied to children from the military age of the upper secondary school. He also said that this subject of the additional year of study had, in fact, been raised earlier for our own students. The three leaders of state secondary schools to be established in Tallinn have said that they are also expecting young people from Russian-speaking schools who are able to study in upper secondary schools, but their Estonian language may not be enough. Then we can offer them a smoother transition. We should therefore consider how to design this additional year of study in the future, perhaps as such, so that it is also applicable to Russian-speaking students in Estonia. Margit Sutrop added that he supported the opportunity offered in the draft for the first year and also that it could be developed for all. Then the question came about what was going on in vocational education. Liina Kersna said that it is possible to take a career year in vocational education and that young people in Ukraine have already taken advantage of this opportunity. They have been introduced to different disciplines and have also been offered language learning. There has also been some way of directing the children of Ukraine who have received, say, the ninth grade certificate in Ukraine, again to the ninth grade so that they can complete this year according to the Estonian curriculum. But the Minister felt that this did not seem reasonable, as a Ukrainian student already has a ninth grade certificate from his own country. The alternative to entering the upper secondary school on an equal footing with others of course exists, but we all understand that this probability, in principle, they do not speak Estonian, after all, is very small. Margit Sutrop also studied the extent to which Estonian and Ukrainian general education curricula overlap. The Minister replied that they would continue to analyse it. The HTM curriculum department compares curricula and this is, of course, a very intensive work. He promised that as soon as this knowledge came, this knowledge would also be shared with the Committee on Culture. The main difference between the curriculum in Estonia and Ukraine is that Ukrainian children go to school at the age of six and their upper secondary school part lasts for two years. That's the biggest difference. Jaak White continued and said that it would still be very important to know as it is, however, a very big change that students basically need. Liina Kersna said that they have discussed these matters with the leaders of schools where there are already so many Ukrainian children in preparation for the draft that such an overview could be obtained. There is no desire to create new instruments. It is the intention of the Ministry to use existing instruments in the law and to extend them as necessary. Jaak Valge, however, admitted that, on the basis of the children of Ukraine who are already attending school, these figures are in fact still very small, but they cannot make larger forecasts or extensions for all refugees. He still came back to what he asked in the introduction to the debate was whether the studies had been carried out and thought that they still needed to be carried out. But that concludes the discussion. As Head of the Commission, Jaak White proposed to appoint Helle-Moonika Helme as the representative of the lead committee, to propose that the draft be put on the plenary agenda by 11 May, or today, and to propose that the first reading be closed. Then we started making procedural decisions. By this time some members of the committee had left: Jaak Juske, Jaanus Karilaid, Signe Kivi, Anneli Ott and Üllar Saaremäe, and we made these procedural decisions with those members of the committee who were present there. ParlaMint-EE_2022-05-11_U533	Today's 11th item is the first reading of the draft law amending the Government Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. [Presentation of the draft bill] reports to Minister of Justice Maris Lauri. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-05-11_U534	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I'll introduce you to the draft law amending the Government of the Republic Act. The current Act of the Government of the Republic with the abbreviation VVS entered into force in 1996. The purpose of this draft is to reflect current regulation and to bring the operating restrictions of a member of the government into line with the Constitution and with the recommendations of the Council of Europe. The proposal introduces a number of changes, some of which are certainly of greater and more important importance, while some are perhaps smaller and, above all, practical. The following is certainly not listed in the order of importance, but there may be a certain degree of gravity. First, the limitation on the activity of a member of the Government of the Republic is brought into line with § 99 of the Constitution, according to which the members of the Government of the Republic must not be in any other state office or be a member of the board or board of directors of a commercial company. It explicitly states that a member of the government may not be a member of the board or council of a public limited company, a private limited company or a commercial cooperative, regardless of whether it is a commercial company owned by a private, state or local government. The Constitution does not prohibit membership of a partnership because it does not have a board of directors or a board of directors, does not prohibit an entrepreneur, carries out scientific and pedagogical work, does not prohibit membership of foundations or non-profit institutions. As a result, § 4 (3) of the Government Act is amended and paragraph 31 of the Government Act is added. The latter indicates that in certain cases it is necessary to inform the Government of its action or intention to take action. This concerns other legal persons [in which the operation] is not prohibited, i.e. foundations, non-profit institutions, members of trusts and partnerships. It is also important that the obligation to notify applies when a member of a government acts or intends to act in such associations outside his or her duties. Perhaps it is perfectly legitimate to act on the board of some legal persons as a result of a post and this does not need to be notified separately. Change number two. The restriction to a member of the Government of the Republic following the end of the term of office of a member of the Government of the Republic arising from the recommendation of GRECO shall be added. The aim of the amendment is to avoid the risk of corruption. On 18 March last year, the Government approved, by decision, guidelines for the prevention of conflicts of interest for ministers and their advisers. According to this, after leaving office, the restriction has been in force for more than a year for the company to become a member of the management or control body of its ministry. With the draft, the government proposes to legitimise the same restriction, but in a somewhat more lenient way. The limitation is set for a period of six months, one year in the Guidelines for the Prevention of Conflict of Interest, and the limitation covers the activity of a private legal person falling within the scope of the Ministry governed by the Minister, when during his term of office the Minister made decisions with a significant impact on the activities of that legal person or when the legal person has contractual relations with the Ministry under his direction. For example, the Minister of Justice may not take up the board of directors or council of AS [Estonian] Prison Industry within six months of leaving office, the Minister of Finance may not go to the board of directors or council of Riigi Kinnisvara AS within six months of leaving office. Thirdly, the regulation on benefits paid at the end of the term of office of a member of the government is amended. The reimbursement shall be made dependent on the time spent in office. If a member of the Government is in office for less than one year, the right to reimbursement shall be limited to the corresponding period of service, but not exceeding six months' salary. If the Minister has been in office for more than a year, the amount of the allowance shall be his six-month salary. It is added that, if the Minister resigns, he or she is entitled to compensation only if he or she has served for at least a year and also for a period of three months. In addition, the draft complements the grounds for non-reimbursement to a member of the government. First, if a member of the government becomes a member of the Riigikogu immediately after his or her dismissal. This concerns both membership of the Riigikogu and the restoration of powers. Second, if a member of the government takes up another elected or appointed post, such as the office of President, after [exemption from the Office], or if he has taken up his duties in an institution of the European Union, for example. The fourth amendment concerns the reimbursement of the housing costs of members of the government, which is made similar to the regulation applicable to members of the Riigikogu. Perhaps in the future it will be foreseen that the housing costs of a member of the government will be reimbursed on request up to 15% of the salary of a member of the government. It is important that a member of the government resides outside the local government unit of the Government of the Republic or of the Ministry that is in charge of it and in neighbouring municipalities. This means that the ministers of the ministries in Tallinn will not receive compensation for living in Tallinn and surrounding municipalities and for living in the municipalities in Tartu and Tartu. Fifthly, it is foreseen that a fixed-term contract of employment will be concluded for a period of five years with the heads of public authorities administered by the government authorities, unless otherwise provided by law. For those managers who currently have an open-ended contract of employment, the deadline starts to run from the entry into force of the amendment to the law. The contracts of employment of the managers of state institutions are, for the most part, fixed-term and are governed by different laws, such as those of the head of the forensic institution, those of the director of the state university of applied sciences, those of the head of the state museum, etc. Currently there is no deadline for, for example, the head of the Registers and Information Systems Centre, the directors of vocational training institutions and some other managers. Those who have a deadline under other laws will not change anything. Why is it made for a fixed term? The reason is that the heads of institutions would then be more motivated to bring about changes and could also encourage rotation between institutions. Sixthly, the role and tasks of the State Chancellery will be specified, for example, by including support for government planning and coordination of the state's strategic planning among its tasks. Seventhly, the differences in the processing of personal data will be added to the State Chancellery, i.e. the current law will be updated in order to ensure more clearly the protection of the rights of the data subject and better legal clarity in the processing of data necessary for the performance of the tasks assigned to the State Chancellery. The draft law still contains a number of changes of a more technical nature due to practical needs. For example, the entry into office of the Government of the Republic will be clarified and it will be deleted that when the government takes office, all members of the government should immediately sign the text of the oath. This is because it may happen that some incoming minister cannot take an oath for very objective reasons. That means he can take his oath and sign some later. The regulation of the resignation of the Minister shall also be specified in such a way that the Prime Minister has the right to reject the resignation within three days. The competence of the Prime Minister is clarified, for example, the obligation for the Prime Minister to present an overview of the activities of the Government of the Republic to the Riigikogu each year. At present, many ministers come here to take stock of their activities. Some ministers derive this responsibility from different laws, but all ministers of the sector are covered by the reviews provided for in the "Estonia 2035." I understand you had one on your agenda today. Now it is added that the Prime Minister should also make an overview. For example, some outdated provisions resulting from technological developments will also be amended. For example, the minutes of the [session] of the government will indicate the place or manner in which the hearing will take place, since the meetings of the government may also take place via e-mail channels. Thank you! I'm ready to answer your questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-05-11_U583	Thank you. According to § 99 of the Constitution: "The members of the Government of the Republic shall not be members of any other government office or be members of the board of directors or board of directors of a commercial company. " Commercial companies are public limited liability companies, private limited liability companies, profit cooperatives, and they are also explicitly mentioned in the law so that the law is read more clearly and clearly. So in this sense, I don't quite understand where you find that with this definition we're going to be narrower than the Constitution says. According to the Constitution, there is still a situation in which it must not be in other public offices and so on. All other activities are permitted, there is no constitutional ban on membership of partnerships, for example, because there is no board of directors or council. The Constitution does not prohibit being an entrepreneur, and we cannot take such steps as may force a minister, for example, to abandon his business. So that principle does not go against the law. If the law introduces certain justified restrictions further, then there is no doubt that they must always be considered and also considered from a constitutional point of view, regardless of which law it is. This has undoubtedly been the case in this case, too, in the setting of these restrictions. For example, we have considered what restrictions to apply to ministers after they have resigned, and this is not an absolute ban, it is a ban within certain limits. This is proportionate, precisely because it is not universal. We have assessed that a six-month limit is essential. We have assessed that it must not go to the institutions and companies on which it has taken decisions. The ban is not for all institutions and public enterprises. It is precisely from such considerations, from constitutional considerations, that these things have been put in place. If the Riigikogu starts discussing this draft in the future committee, the committee can without doubt assess and consider whether this proposed balance point is appropriate or not. I'm sure there's always discussion points, but we found it acceptable. So I think, given Estonia's small size, especially in terms of the number of people, imposing too rigid restrictions, imposing very extensive restrictions will limit people's other fundamental rights and freedoms, and eventually it will also hinder Estonian society, entrepreneurship and so on. For example, if we were to impose a restriction on ministers not to manage businesses at all for a period of time, it would, in my opinion, be very clearly across the border. There are countries where these restrictions are much tougher, but this is certainly due to the fact that the countries are more extensive, the variation of jobs is wider, the person has more opportunities to earn a living after leaving this job, including decent service, and given Estonia's small size, there may not always be so many jobs. So we undoubtedly considered the different places to put this balance in.
